SSHCustom-VPNChain — Session Handoff
Repo
GoodyOG/sshcustom-vpnchain — already cloned at /projects/sandbox/sshcustom-vpnchain

Current version
v2.0.4 (released) — APK and ZIP at https://github.com/GoodyOG/sshcustom-vpnchain/releases/tag/v2.0.4

What this project is
A Magisk/KernelSU root module + companion Android app for an ARM64 Android device. The owner has no active mobile data plan — the SSH tunnel exploits a carrier free-host loophole via payload injection or SNI spoofing. All device TCP traffic is routed through the tunnel via iptables (no VpnService). The app is the control interface.

Tech stack:

Module scripts: module/scripts/ssh.service, ssh.iptables, ssh.tool, module/settings.ini, module/service.sh
Go daemon: daemon/ — GOOS=android GOARCH=arm64 CGO_ENABLED=0 static binary
Android app: Kotlin + Jetpack Compose + miuix 0.7.2 (NOT 0.9.x — requires compileSdk 35, 0.9.x needs SDK 37 which CI runners don't have)
CI: .github/workflows/build.yml — builds daemon + tun2proxy + module ZIP + release APK
What was just built (v2.0.4 is live, user has it installed)

Outstanding work — DO THIS NEXT, wait for user's "go"
1. ● CRITICAL — DNS fix (tunnel won't connect without this)
The exact error from logs:

[tunnel] connecting to www.bc.gam:80 mode=direct
[tunnel] connect failed: transport dial: dial tcp: lookup www.bc.gam on [::1]:53: read udp [::1]:60814->[::1]:53: read: connection refused
Root cause: The Go daemon uses Android's system DNS stub resolver at [::1]:53. When ipv6=false is set in settings.ini (which disables IPv6 system-wide via sysctl), [::1] becomes unreachable. The daemon cannot resolve the SSH server hostname.

The fix: Create daemon/internal/dnsx/dnsx.go — a custom DNS resolver that:

Reads carrier IPv4 DNS servers from Android system properties (getprop net.dns1, net.dns2, dhcp.rmnet_data0.dns1 etc.)
Makes raw UDP DNS queries directly to those servers (bypassing dnsproxyd)
Has a 5-minute in-process cache
Falls back to 8.8.8.8:53 and 1.1.1.1:53 if no carrier DNS found
A dnsx.go file was already written and is on disk at /projects/sandbox/sshcustom-vpnchain/daemon/internal/dnsx/dnsx.go in the previous session but was NOT yet wired into dialTransport() in client.go or committed/pushed.

What still needs to happen:

In daemon/internal/ssh/client.go, inside dialTransport() for ModeDirect and ModeSNI and ModeSNIHTTPProxy: instead of net.Dialer{}.DialContext(), use dnsx.New().DialContext() which does the Android-aware DNS lookup first then dials the resolved IPv4 address
Rebuild daemon, push, tag, release
File to edit: daemon/internal/ssh/client.go — dialTransport() function, all 3 cases. Replace:

d := &net.Dialer{Timeout: timeout}
conn, err := d.DialContext(ctx, "tcp", addr)
With:

resolver := dnsx.New()
conn, err := resolver.DialContext(ctx, "tcp", addr)
(The addr here is fmt.Sprintf("%s:%d", cfg.Host, cfg.Port) — the resolver will split host/port, resolve the host using carrier DNS, then dial the IPv4 result.)

Also add import: "github.com/GoodyOG/SSHCustom_Magisk/internal/dnsx"

Hello! I've now read the actual daemon sources (main.go, client.go, config.go) and all three lifecycle scripts (ssh.service, ssh.tool, ssh.iptables), so my analysis below is grounded in the real code rather than the handoff. Here's the full breakdown of those logs.

Line-by-line log analysis
Phase 1 — Boot (12:55), idle daemon
boot service started → boot ready (waited 15s) → starting daemon in idle mode
autostart disabled — daemon idle at 127.0.0.1:9190
service: daemon started pid=7821 flags=... --idle
[http] listening on 127.0.0.1:9190
This is service.sh calling ssh.service start-idle because the autostart marker isn't set. The daemon launches with --idle, which (per main.go) skips tunnelLoop entirely — it only brings up the two control APIs. So at boot, nothing tries to connect. That's correct/expected behavior.

About 9190: that's the daemon's hardcoded local HTTP control API — httpSrv.Addr = "127.0.0.1:9190" in main.go. It's bound to loopback only and is completely separate from your proxy ports (SOCKS 1081, redir 9799, tproxy 9899). The companion app talks to the daemon over this port (plus the unix socket sshcustomd.sock). Seeing 9190 is normal — not a misconfiguration.

Phase 2 — You hit Start (13:05), active daemon
service starting...
service: daemon started pid=22094 flags=-c ... -w ...   (no --idle)
service: API ready
The app sent control start → handleControl → ssh.service start → start_daemon. It detected the old daemon was in --idle mode, killed pid 7821, and relaunched as pid 22094 without --idle, so now tunnelLoop is running.

ssh.tool[192]: can't create /dev/blkio/sshcustom/blkio.weight: Permission denied
Cosmetic. start_service calls ssh.tool blkio. Your kernel exposes a blkio cgroup mount at /dev/blkio, but it won't let a new sshcustom sub-cgroup be created/written (SELinux or restricted v1 controller). The script tries to suppress this with 2>/dev/null, but it leaks anyway because of a shell quirk: in cmd > file 2>/dev/null, the > file redirection is set up before 2>/dev/null takes effect, so when opening the file fails the shell prints the error to the still-original stderr. Harmless — blkio is a "nice-to-have" I/O priority tweak — but it's noise I can silence in this release if you want (reorder the redirection / pre-check writability).

cap: tproxy4=true tproxy6=true conntrack=true connmark=true ipset=false ip6_nat=false
iptables: mode=redirect dns=redirect quic=disable ipv6=false
ssh.iptables enable ran. Your kernel supports TPROXY + conntrack + connmark; no ipset, no IPv6 NAT. Current mode is redirect, and dns=redirect is the current default we're about to flip to disable.

Phase 3 — The actual failure (the core bug)
[tunnel] connecting to www.bc.gam:80 mode=direct
[tunnel] connect failed: ... lookup www.bc.gam on [::1]:53: read udp ...->[::1]:53: read: connection refused
This is dialTransport() → ModeDirect → net.Dialer.DialContext("tcp", "www.bc.gam:80"). Go's pure-Go resolver (CGO is disabled) has no usable /etc/resolv.conf on Android, so it falls back to localhost nameservers 127.0.0.1:53 and [::1]:53. Nothing is listening there (Android resolves DNS through netd/bionic, not a local UDP:53 socket), so → connection refused. This is the blocker.

The error mutates — and that proves the IPv6 theory
12:05:42  read udp [::1]:53: read: connection refused
12:05:48  dial udp [::1]:53: connect: cannot assign requested address
12:06:00  dial udp [::1]:53: connect: network is unreachable
Watch how the same lookup degrades. The first attempt (connection refused) happened before ssh.iptables enable finished. Because ipv6=false and network_mode=redirect, the enable path hit the else → disable_ipv6 branch, which runs sysctl net.ipv6.conf.all.disable_ipv6=1 and adds ip -6 rule add unreachable. After that, [::1] itself is gone → cannot assign requested address → network is unreachable. This is direct, observable confirmation that disabling IPv6 system-wide is what kills the [::1]:53 resolver path. Even before IPv6 was disabled it was already failing (refused), so the daemon could never resolve regardless — disabling IPv6 just changes the symptom.

The false-positive non-zero exit
redirect: rules applied (port=9799)
iptables: iptables -w 100 REDIRECT rules applied
service: iptables enable returned non-zero — check log for details
The rules did apply. The non-zero exit is exactly the bug we planned to fix: the last statement in the enable|renew) case is [ "${1}" = "renew" ] && log Info .... When $1 is enable, the [ ... = "renew" ] test evaluates false → returns exit 1 → that becomes the script's exit code → ssh.service logs the scary (but meaningless) warning. exit 0 at the end of the case fixes it.

The "disabling" line + retries that never stop — a real secondary issue
13:06  iptables: disabling — cleaning all rules
13:06  iptables: all rules removed — device in clean state
12:06:24 [tunnel] connecting to www.bc.gam:80 ...   (and continues every 30s until 13:12+)
Two things to know here:

Timestamps are mixed. The shell log() uses date '+%H:%M' = device local time (13:06), while the Go daemon's log package is emitting UTC (12:06). Your device is UTC+1, so 12:06:24 Go = 13:06:24 local. That ~1h offset is purely cosmetic but makes the merged log very confusing to read. I can fix it by forcing the daemon to local time (or stamping a TZ) — worth doing since you're reading these logs to debug.

The daemon kept retrying for ~7 minutes after iptables were torn down. Putting the timeline in real local time: iptables were disabled at ~13:06, yet pid 22094 keeps attempting connects at 13:06:24, 13:06:54, 13:07:24… The only code path that runs ssh.iptables disable here is ssh.service stop (the daemon's own teardown only runs after a successful connect, which never happened). So a stop was triggered, but the daemon process survived and kept looping. That points to a coordination gap between the stop flow and the tunnel loop — the rules get removed but the daemon isn't actually killed (or the app issued a partial/disable-only path). This isn't on your task list, and it's not the blocker, but it's a real bug. I'd want to read the app's control flow (Repository.kt / MainViewModel.kt) and double-check ssh.service stop's kill targeting to confirm. Flagging it — your call whether to fold a fix into v2.0.5.

Root cause + does the fix actually solve it?
Root cause: the daemon can't resolve www.bc.gam because Go's resolver falls back to [::1]:53/127.0.0.1:53, which has no listener, and IPv6 being disabled makes it worse. The custom dnsx resolver (query carrier DNS from getprop directly over IPv4 UDP) bypasses this entirely. That part is solid.

One critical caveat you should know before we ship: www.bc.gam is a bug host — .gam is not a real TLD. Public resolvers (8.8.8.8 / 1.1.1.1) will return NXDOMAIN for it, so the fallback servers are useless for this specific host (still worth keeping for real hostnames). It can only resolve if your carrier's own DNS has an entry for it — which is exactly why dnsx must prioritize net.dns* / dhcp.rmnet_data0.dns*. The good news: carriers almost always leave their DNS reachable on zero balance (for captive portals), so this usually works. But note a second layer: in direct mode on port 80 with payload_enabled=false, there's no SNI or Host-header trick — the bypass then depends purely on the resolved destination IP being zero-rated by the carrier. If carrier DNS resolves the host but the connection still fails after the DNS fix, the next lever is payload injection or SNI mode. I'll make dnsx log which server answered and the resolved IP, so we can see exactly what's happening on the next run.

Confirmed scope for v2.0.5 (waiting on your "go")

The actual bug: the profile is saved, but never written to settings.ini
The smoking gun in the log:

2026/05/30 14:38:30 [tunnel] ssh_host/ssh_user not configured — waiting
...repeated every 10s...
This comes from the daemon's tunnelLoop: if cfg.SSHHost == "" || cfg.SSHUser == "". The daemon (pid 18897) started in active mode (no --idle), read settings.ini, and found ssh_host/ssh_user empty. So your profile's credentials never reached settings.ini. You're right that the app isn't writing it — and I found exactly why.

Two-layer storage in this app:

Profiles are saved as JSON in SharedPreferences (this works fine).
SSH credentials only get written into settings.ini (what the daemon actually reads) by applyProfileToSettings().
Now look at MainViewModel.saveProfile():

_profiles.value = current
repo.saveProfiles(current)
if (profile.id == _activeProfileId.value) {   // ← the trap
    applyProfileToSettings(profile)
}
applyProfileToSettings() is called only if the profile you just saved is already the active one. When you create a new profile, its fresh UUID is never equal to the current _activeProfileId (which is "" on first run), so applyProfileToSettings is skipped entirely — settings.ini keeps its empty ssh_host="".

The only other place that writes credentials is selectProfile(), which fires when you tap the "Select" button on a profile card (the one that shows next to "Edit/Delete" when a profile isn't active). So today the required sequence is: Add Profile → tap Select → Start. If you go Add Profile → Home → Start (the obvious flow), the daemon runs with no credentials. First-time users hit this 100% of the time.

You can confirm this right now without any new build: go to the Profiles tab, tap Select on your profile so it shows "● Active", then hit Start. It should get past "not configured" and move on to the connect/DNS stage. That verifies the diagnosis.

What I plan to do (waiting for your go)
1. Fix profile application (MainViewModel.saveProfile) — auto-activate a newly created profile (and whenever none is active) so its SSH fields are written to settings.ini immediately:

val isNew = idx < 0
...
if (isNew || _activeProfileId.value.isBlank()) {
    _activeProfileId.value = profile.id
    repo.saveActiveProfileId(profile.id)
    applyProfileToSettings(profile)        // writes ssh_host/user/etc.
} else if (profile.id == _activeProfileId.value) {
    applyProfileToSettings(profile)        // re-apply edits to the active profile
}
Result: create a profile → it becomes Active → credentials written → Start works. Editing the active profile re-writes it; editing a non-active profile just persists it (you still pick it with Select). This is the real fix.

2. Belt-and-suspenders (recommended, low risk): also re-apply the active profile's SSH fields to settings.ini at the start of startTunnel(), so "whatever profile shows as Active is guaranteed written" right before the daemon reads it. This kills the whole class of "stale/empty settings.ini" bugs. I'll include this unless you'd rather keep startTunnel minimal.

3. Remove the "Control" log tab — control.log is never created by anything (the daemon writes sshcustom.log = Core; boot writes boot.log = Boot), so the tab is always empty. I'll drop "control" to "Control" from LOG_TABS in LogsScreen.kt and the matching "control" key in MainViewModel.LOG_PATHS, leaving Core and Boot only.

The updated log is illuminating — and it shows real progress. Here's the analysis.

Good news first: two of the three blockers are now gone
14:54:33 [tunnel] connecting to www.bc.game:80 mode=direct
14:54:34 [dnsx] resolved www.bc.game via 8.8.8.8:53 -> [104.18.29.120 104.18.28.120]
14:54:34 [dnsx] www.bc.game -> 104.18.29.120 (dial 104.18.29.120:80)
The profile is now being written to settings.ini (host/user are present — that's why it's connecting instead of "not configured"). Tapping Select did exactly what I predicted, confirming the saveProfile diagnosis.
The DNS resolver works — [dnsx] resolved the host and dialed it. The TCP connection itself succeeded (no dial error).
The new error — and what it actually means
14:54:34 [tunnel] connect failed: ssh handshake: ssh: handshake failed: ssh: overflow reading version string
overflow reading version string is a golang.org/x/crypto/ssh error. After the TCP connect, the SSH client sends its banner (SSH-2.0-...) and waits to read the server's banner back. It read 255+ bytes that were not a valid SSH identification line, so it gave up. In plain terms: whatever you connected to is not an SSH server.

And the log tells us exactly what it connected to: 104.18.29.120 / 104.18.28.120. Those are Cloudflare IPs (104.16.0.0/12). www.bc.game is a real Cloudflare-fronted website. So in direct mode the daemon opened a plain TCP socket to Cloudflare's web server on port 80, said "SSH-2.0-Go", and Cloudflare answered with HTTP/garbage → handshake overflow. There is no SSH daemon at that address, and there never will be.

The root issue: this is a method/config problem, not a code bug
This is the key thing to understand about carrier free-host bypass: the bug host (www.bc.game) is only meant to be shown to the carrier — as a TLS SNI or an HTTP Host header — so the carrier zero-rates the traffic. The TCP connection still has to actually terminate at your SSH server.

In direct mode there's no SNI and no Host trick — it just connects straight to whatever you typed as the host. You typed the bug host, so it connected to the bug host's real web server. That can't work. The correct setups are:

SNI / SSL mode: your SSH server runs behind TLS (e.g., stunnel on 443). Profile: host = your real server IP/domain, port = 443, mode = SNI, SNI Host = www.bc.game. The carrier inspects the TLS ClientHello, sees the zero-rated SNI, and lets it through; SSH runs inside the TLS tunnel to your server.
HTTP payload / proxy mode: connect to your real SSH server or a proxy on port 80, then inject an HTTP request whose Host: is www.bc.game. The carrier sees the bug host in cleartext and zero-rates; the server/proxy bridges to SSH. Here the bug host goes in the payload, and host is your real server/proxy.
In every working variant, host (the dial target) is your real SSH server, and www.bc.game lives in the SNI or the payload — never as the dial target.

A secondary observation worth noting
The resolver answered via 8.8.8.8 (the public fallback), not carrier DNS — meaning readCarrierDNS() found nothing in getprop this run, so it fell through to public DNS. That's fine here because www.bc.game is a real domain. But if you ever use a fake bug host (like the original www.bc.gam), public DNS will NXDOMAIN it and only carrier DNS could answer. Just flagging it for later.

Do you have your own SSH server / VPS? What's its real host or IP and SSH port?
What does your carrier actually zero-rate — does your working bypass on other apps (HTTP Injector, etc.) use SNI/SSL or HTTP payload injection?
Is your SSH server plain SSH, or behind stunnel/TLS (needed for SNI mode)?
Once I know that, I can tell you the exact profile config — and if the app's editor is missing a field that makes this clean (e.g., a dedicated "real host vs bug host" split), I can add it.

This is,the configuration, Payload GET / HTTP/1.1[crlf]Host: ionosuk.techychi.xyz[crlf]Connection: Upgrade[crlf]Upgrade: Websocket[crlf][crlf] this is what I've used on my previous modules, and it's what I use on normal vpn apps, and they all connect successfully. Analyze agian to see what's going on. get back to me.

This is the missing piece. Your payload tells me exactly what technique you're using, and it points straight at a real daemon bug. Let me walk through it.

What your config actually is
GET / HTTP/1.1[crlf]Host: ionosuk.techychi.xyz[crlf]Connection: Upgrade[crlf]Upgrade: Websocket[crlf][crlf]
This is the classic SSH-over-WebSocket / HTTP-Upgrade injection method (HTTP Injector / HTTP Custom / NapsternetV style). The flow that should happen:

TCP-connect to the front host (www.bc.game:80, on Cloudflare).
Send that HTTP Upgrade: Websocket request (the carrier zero-rates based on it / the Host).
The server/CDN answers HTTP/1.1 101 Switching Protocols + headers, ending in a blank line (\r \r ).
Only after that does the socket become a raw tunnel, and the real SSH banner (SSH-2.0-…) starts flowing — then the SSH handshake runs.
What the daemon is doing wrong
Look at the daemon's direct-mode payload code (client.go):

if cfg.PayloadEnabled && cfg.Payload != "" {
    p := substitutePayload(...)
    conn.Write([]byte(p))   // sends the WebSocket upgrade
}
return conn, nil            // ← hands socket straight to SSH, never reads the 101 response
It sends your payload but never reads the 101 Switching Protocols reply. So it hands the socket to the SSH client immediately, and the SSH client reads the HTTP response as if it were the SSH banner.

That's the exact cause of your error, down to the wording. Go's SSH library skips non-SSH- lines while reading the banner, but only up to 255 total bytes. A Cloudflare 101/response easily exceeds 255 bytes of headers (Server, Date, Cf-Ray, etc.) before any SSH- line appears — so it bails with precisely:

ssh: handshake failed: ssh: overflow reading version string
This is why your identical payload works in other VPN apps: they consume the 101 response (read up to \r \r ) before starting SSH. This daemon doesn't. It's a clear, fixable bug — not a config mistake on your end.

Side note: sni_http_proxy mode in the daemon does read a response, but it only accepts 200 — it would also reject a 101 Switching Protocols. So both payload paths need the same correction.

The fix I'll make (daemon)
After writing the payload, read the HTTP response byte-by-byte up to \r \r (so I don't accidentally swallow the SSH banner that comes right after), accept 101 or 2xx as success, then hand the upgraded socket to SSH. I'll also add [payload] log lines (bytes sent, and the server's status line) so the next log shows us exactly what the server replied — and apply the 101-accept fix to the proxy mode too.

Just to point out, it should be able to use any type of Payload., just like normal vpn app http custom, http injector does, so it shouldn't be limited to a specific kind,of Payload, any type of Payload should work. Analyze and report back.

Understood — and that's the right call. Let me refine the analysis so the fix is generic, the way HTTP Injector / HTTP Custom behave, rather than WebSocket-specific.

Correcting my earlier plan
My previous proposal ("accept 101, also accept 200") was too narrow — it would just trade one hardcoded assumption for another. Your point is correct: the payload engine should be method-agnostic. A CONNECT payload, a WebSocket upgrade, a GET/POST host-injection, a split/multi-request payload, or even a raw non-HTTP prefix should all work. The daemon shouldn't care what the payload is.

What "generic" actually requires (how the injector apps do it)
In HTTP Injector / Custom, the payload is just raw bytes with tokens, sent to the remote. The only thing the client does afterward is: drain whatever HTTP acknowledgement the server/CDN sends, then treat the socket as a raw tunnel and start SSH. The current daemon skips that drain entirely, so SSH eats the HTTP reply and overflows. The generic fix has three parts:

Drain any HTTP response, regardless of status. After writing the payload, if the server replies with an HTTP/... block, consume the full status line + headers up to the blank \r \r . Don't validate the code — 101, 200, 302, whatever. Just log the status line for visibility and let the SSH handshake be the real success/failure test. This is exactly how injectors behave (they're lenient about the response).

Handle multiple responses and the no-response case. Some payloads produce two HTTP responses (a "front" request then the real tunnel request); some produce none (a pure raw prefix where the SSH banner comes straight back). So: keep consuming response blocks only while the incoming data starts with HTTP/; the moment it doesn't (e.g., it's SSH-2.0-…), stop and hand off to SSH. With a bounded read deadline so a silent server can't hang us — if nothing HTTP-looking arrives, we just proceed.

Don't lose the SSH banner (the tricky part). The upstream SSH server usually sends its SSH-2.0-… banner immediately after the upgrade, so it often arrives glued onto the tail of the HTTP response in the same TCP read. If I naively buffer the response I'd swallow those banner bytes. So I'll wrap the socket in a small prefix-buffer connection that replays any already-read tunnel bytes to the SSH layer first, then passes through to the raw socket. This guarantees zero byte loss for any payload shape.

Net effect: the payload field becomes a transparent pipe — you paste the same payload that works in HTTP Custom, and the daemon sends it, clears the server's acknowledgement, and starts SSH on the raw stream. No payload type is privileged.

Tokens (the other half of "any payload")
Send-side, your payload uses [crlf], which the daemon already substitutes (along with [host], [port], [cr], [lf]). Injector apps also support tokens like [ua], [host_port], [protocol], and DPI-evasion tokens like [split]/[delay] (which chunk the payload). Your current payload doesn't use those, so I won't add them blindly — but tell me if any of your payloads do and I'll wire the common ones in. This generic engine is also mode-agnostic, so I'll route sni_http_proxy through the same drain (today it only accepts 200, which would reject a WS payload).

Cleaned up. Tunnel works, all apps route through it — great milestone. Here's my extensive analysis and the full plan for each item. No code changed yet; holding for your go.

What I discovered from the box APK (com.box.app)
Decompiled with jadx. It's the same core stack as ours (Jetpack Compose + okhttp + kotlinx-serialization), but a few architectural choices explain the behaviors you want:

It runs a persistent foreground service (BoxPersistentNotificationService, FOREGROUND_SERVICE_SPECIAL_USE) with a notification channel box_persistent_status. The service holds the live state and computes up/down speed itself (I can see the bytes*1000/dt math inside it). Because a privileged/long-lived service owns the state, the UI never shows a stale "Stopped" when you return — it reads from the running service.
Notification + Quick Settings tile actions: ACTION_STOP, ACTION_RESTART, ACTION_RELOAD (and start) — start/stop/restart straight from the shade.
State labels: Starting / Running / Stopping / Stopped + "Connected time" (uptime) — exactly the always-visible state machine you described.
compileSdk 37 (vs our 35) — confirms why we're pinned to miuix 0.7.2.
It ships a TextMate code editor (assets/textmate for ini/shell/yaml/json) — that's its in-app config-file editor.
Biggest takeaway: box computes net speed and metrics inside the privileged service, not in a non-root UI process. That's the root reason our Home metrics read 0 (details below), and the foreground service is why it never flashes "Stopped."

1. The ~10s connect delay — root cause
From your log, timestamps tell the whole story:

15:53:26 daemon started
15:53:29 [tunnel] connecting        ← 3s of doing nothing
15:53:30 connected
15:53:30→36 iptables runs TWICE, then…
15:53:36 [socks5]/[transparent] listening   ← 6s after connect
Three separate causes, all fixable:

Fixed 3s startup delay — tunnelLoop waits delay = 3s before the first connect attempt. Pure dead time.
iptables is applied twice — ssh.service start runs ssh.iptables enable, and the daemon also runs it after connecting (you can see the redirect: rules applied block appear twice). Double the capability-probe + cleanup + apply work.
Listeners start after the (synchronous) iptables run — the SOCKS5/transparent listeners only bind at :36 because the daemon blocks on ssh.iptables enable first. So for ~6s, traffic is already being redirected to port 9799 with nothing listening there.
Plan: (a) make the first connect attempt immediate (keep backoff only for retries); (b) remove the iptables enable from ssh.service and let the daemon own iptables (enable after connect, disable on failure/stop); (c) start the SOCKS5/transparent listeners before applying iptables so the redirect target is live instantly. Expected: ~10s → ~3-4s.

2. Home metrics all read 0 — per-metric root cause
I checked the daemon and the JSON field names match the app, so it's not a parsing bug — the daemon simply never produces these numbers, and the app reads two of them from a place it can't access:

CPU 0.0% — the daemon's metricsLoop only updates memory; cpu_percent is never computed. So it's always 0.
Pool (e.g. 0/8) — channel_pool_available is set once at connect (when the pool hasn't warmed yet) and never updated; the pre-warm fills asynchronously afterward, so the UI shows a stale count.
"X active" (connections) — the active-connection counter exists but nothing ever increments it (the SOCKS5/transparent handlers don't touch it). Always 0.
Net Speed 0.0 — computed in the app by reading /proc/net/dev, but the app is a non-root process and Android/SELinux restricts that, so it reads nothing → 0. (Box solves this by computing speed in its root service — exactly as I found.)
WAN IP "—" — the app calls GET /api/v1/network/public-ip, but that endpoint doesn't exist in the daemon (only health/status/control/autostart/config are registered) → 404 → "—".
Mem actually works (your status line showed mem=7 MB).
Plan (daemon-side, where it belongs):

Compute CPU% in metricsLoop from /proc/self/stat utime+stime deltas.
Update pool avail every tick from the live client's PoolStats().
Add an active-connections counter on the SSH client; increment/decrement in the SOCKS5 + transparent handlers.
Compute net speed in the daemon (root, reads /proc/net/dev fine), expose up_kbps/down_kbps in the status JSON, and switch the app to read those instead of /proc/net/dev.
Add a real /api/v1/network/public-ip endpoint that dials an IP-echo service through the SSH tunnel and returns the tunnel-side IP (+country), matching what the app already parses.
3. UI — return-from-background shows "Stopped" for a second
The status flow uses SharingStarted.WhileSubscribed(5_000): when you background the app for >5s, polling stops and the StateFlow resets to its default (connected=false = "Stopped"). On resume it shows that default until the first poll (~1s) lands.

Plan: convert status/netspeed/wan to a ViewModel-owned polling job writing to retained MutableStateFlows (alive for the VM's lifetime), and do an immediate poll on resume. The last known state is shown instantly — no flash. (The box-style foreground service is the heavier "proper" version of this; I'd suggest the lightweight fix now and we can add a foreground service + notification/QS-tile later if you want shade controls.)

4. UI — always-visible Start / Stop / Restart with spinners
Today Home shows either a single "Start" or a Reload/Stop/Restart row.

Plan: always render Start, Stop, Restart in one row; enable/disable each based on state; the in-progress action shows a spinner + "Starting…/Stopping…/Restarting…" (driven by the existing isLoading + tunnel-state override), matching the box pattern. I'll verify miuix's progress indicator; if it lacks one I'll use a small rotating indicator.

5. Settings — remove "Force iptables cleanup"
Plan: remove the Developer section (the SuperArrow) and its onForceCleanup wiring. Since Stop is always visible and the daemon now cleans iptables on stop and on connect failure, the manual button is redundant.

6. Clean up iptables when connecting fails
Tied to #1: once the daemon owns iptables (enable only after a successful connect), I'll also run ssh.iptables disable in the connect-failure branch of the retry loop. So a failed/looping connect can never leave stale redirect rules that black-hole traffic.

Proposed release: v2.0.7
Daemon: immediate first connect; own iptables (enable-after-connect, disable-on-fail/stop); listeners before iptables; CPU%, pool, active-conns, net-speed metrics; new public-IP endpoint.
ssh.service: drop the redundant iptables enable (keep disable as safety).
App: always-visible buttons + spinners; retained status flows (no flash); read net-speed/WAN from daemon; remove Force-cleanup.
Deployment: daemon + scripts change → reflash ZIP; app changes → reinstall APK. So v2.0.7 needs both, again.
That's everything. Tell me go (or trim the scope) and I'll implement it all in one v2.0.7 and release the APK + ZIP.

2. TPROXY connects but nothing works — root cause found
16:43 iptables: mode=tproxy ... tproxy: rules applied (port=9899)
[socks5] listening on 127.0.0.1:1081
[transparent] listening on 0.0.0.0:9799
The SSH tunnel is up, and iptables sends intercepted traffic to TPROXY port 9899 — but the daemon never starts a listener on 9899. It only starts SOCKS5 (1081) and the REDIRECT-style transparent server (9799). So in tproxy mode every connection is routed to a dead port → total black-hole. The existing transparent server also can't serve TPROXY anyway: it reads SO_ORIGINAL_DST (a REDIRECT/nat thing), whereas TPROXY needs an IP_TRANSPARENT socket and takes the original destination from the accepted socket's local address.

Reality check: even fully implemented, TPROXY over plain SSH only helps TCP (SSH can't carry UDP without a udpgw). So TCP-wise it's essentially equivalent to the redirect mode that already works. I'll give you options below.

3. TUN connects but nothing works — root cause found
[tun2proxy] started pid=12937
error: unexpected argument '--device' found
  tip: a similar argument exists: '--daemonize'
Usage: tun2proxy --proxy <URL> --daemonize
The daemon launches tun2proxy with --device tun0 --proxy ... --dns over-tcp, but the tun2proxy binary CI built rejects --device and exits instantly. So no TUN traffic is ever serviced. Two problems: (a) wrong CLI flags — this build wants --tun (not --device), and (b) CI doesn't pin a tun2proxy version (it clones main), so the CLI can drift and break again. tun/tun_udpgw is the mode that actually adds value (full IP + UDP via udpgw), so it's worth fixing properly.

Proposed plans (pick the scope)
A. Status card (app) — replace the dot with a box-style status icon + label + uptime, color-coded per state. Folds naturally into the buttons/Home rework already planned.

B. TUN fix (daemon + CI) — correct the tun2proxy invocation (--tun, proper --dns/--setup flags), and pin tun2proxy to a specific release tag in build.yml so the CLI is stable; I'll verify the exact flags against that tag. This makes tun (TCP+DNS) and tun_udpgw (adds UDP) actually work.

C. TPROXY — your call:

C1 (recommended): implement a real TCP TPROXY listener (IP_TRANSPARENT, original-dst from local addr) on port 9899 so the mode isn't a dead end. Note UDP still needs tun_udpgw.
C2 (simpler): since it gives no real benefit over redirect for SSH, drop/hide tproxy and keep redirect as the TCP default + tun_udpgw for UDP. Less code, less confusion.
D. Everything from the previous report (still pending your go): connect-speed fixes (immediate first attempt, single daemon-owned iptables, listeners-before-iptables, cleanup-on-failure), Home metrics (CPU/pool/active-conns/net-speed/WAN-IP), always-visible Start/Stop/Restart + spinners, remove Force-cleanup, no background "Stopped" flash.

My recommendation for one v2.0.7: do A + B + D, and C1 for tproxy (or C2 if you'd rather not maintain a TPROXY listener). All of it is daemon + scripts + app, so it'll need a ZIP reflash + APK reinstall.

I've reviewed the updated logs and the SSH client code. There's a lot here, and the logs reveal the single most important issue. Here's my extensive analysis — no code changed yet; I want your decisions on a few things before implementing.

The headline finding: your SSH connection is dropping every ~20 seconds
This is the root cause of almost everything you're feeling ("apps think I disconnect for a sec"). From your redirect log:

17:58:53 [tunnel] connected
17:59:14 [tunnel] SSH connection lost: EOF      ← 21 seconds later
17:59:14 [tunnel] tearing down → iptables disabling → all rules removed
17:59:17 [tunnel] connecting … 
17:59:18 [tunnel] connected → rules re-applied
The server (the bc.game WebSocket bridge → your SSH) is closing the connection with EOF after ~21s, then the daemon rebuilds it. During that ~4s rebuild, the iptables rules are removed and re-added — so every app's traffic blips. That's exactly the "offline for a sec" you see in both redirect and tproxy.

Why it drops at ~21s: two compounding causes:

SSH keepalive is too slow. It's set to 30s, but the WebSocket/bug-host path is being torn down at ~20s of idle — before our first keepalive fires. A ~10s keepalive would generate traffic early enough to keep the path alive.
The channel pool is hammering the server. On connect, the pool tries to pre-open 8 SSH channels to 127.0.0.1:7 (a discard port) on the server. That port refuses, so it retries a failed channel-open every 500ms forever. Many SSH/bridge servers drop a client that floods failed channel requests — this may be causing the 20s EOF, and it's why the pool is stuck (next item).
Fix plan: drop the SSH keepalive to ~10s, and remove the channel-pool warming entirely (it's broken and likely harmful). If the connection stays up, the blips disappear and WAN resolves.

"0/8 channels" — what it is and why it's stuck (your explicit ask)
The "channel pool" is a pre-warming optimization: the daemon tries to keep 8 SSH channels pre-opened so the first connection through the tunnel doesn't pay channel-setup latency. To warm them, it opens throwaway channels to 127.0.0.1:7 on the server side. Your server refuses port 7 (nothing listens there), so no channel is ever added → it's permanently stuck at 0/8, while it pointlessly retries twice a second. It does not affect real traffic (actual connections open their own channels on demand — that's why "active" works). It's misleading and, as above, possibly causing your disconnects. Recommendation: remove the channel pool feature (removes the "0/8" confusion and the server hammering). The "active" count is the genuinely useful number.

Home: WAN / Net Speed / Resources show nothing
In v2.0.7 these are all produced by the daemon. Since your "active connections" number updates, the app is reading fresh status — so the daemon's metrics loop is running. A few things are going on:

WAN is almost certainly a casualty of the flapping above: it's resolved through the tunnel on connect and cleared on every disconnect. With the link dropping every ~20s, the lookup never finishes before it's wiped. Fix the disconnects → WAN resolves.
CPU will legitimately read ~0.0% (the daemon is near-idle) — it's not broken, it just looks it.
Net Speed is daemon-computed in v2.0.7 (up_kbps/down_kbps), but only the v2.0.7 APK reads those fields.
To pin this precisely, can you confirm Settings → About → App version reads 2.0.7, and if possible paste the output of:

curl http://127.0.0.1:9190/api/v1/status
That JSON shows instantly whether the daemon is emitting mem_rss_mb, cpu_percent, up_kbps, down_kbps — isolating daemon vs. app in one shot. My strong suspicion is the disconnect flapping (for WAN) plus needing the v2.0.7 APK (for net speed).

Returning from background still flashes "disconnected"
My v2.0.7 fix (keep the status flow alive) only helps if the app process survives. Your device is HyperOS/MIUI-style, which aggressively kills backgrounded apps. When you return, the process cold-starts, the ViewModel is recreated with a default (disconnected) state, and you see it for ~1s until the first poll. The box app avoids this with a persistent foreground service (the notification) that keeps the process and state alive. Proper fix: add a lightweight foreground service (like box) + seed the last-known state from storage on cold start. This is a bigger change — want it in?

tun2proxy — and do you even need it?
Progress: with v0.8.1 + correct flags it now starts, but:

[tun2proxy] started pid=16276 (tun=tun0)
ERROR tun2proxy_bin: main loop error: invalid configuration
Without --setup, v0.8.1 won't self-configure the tun device on Android and bails with InvalidConfig. I could fix it with --setup + bypassing the server IP, but here's the important part:

For an SSH-only tunnel, tun2proxy adds nothing. SSH can only carry TCP. tun2proxy's whole value is capturing all IP traffic including UDP — but UDP needs a SOCKS proxy that supports UDP, which an SSH tunnel doesn't (that's what tun_udpgw + a udpgw server is for, which you don't run). So redirect/tproxy already cover everything SSH can do. My recommendation: drop tun/tun_udpgw from the public module entirely — remove the tun2proxy binary (saves ~2 MB), delete the broken modes, and keep REDIRECT (default) + TPROXY. Cleaner, smaller, nothing lost.

Your direction: public SSH module, personal VPN chain separate
Understood and agreed — and it fits the above. For the public module (anyone can use): SSH tunnel only. I'll strip the VPN-chain remnants (the "VPN Chain — Coming soon" row in Settings, any vpnchain/ configs/scripts) so the public release is purely the SSH tunnel. Your OpenVPN/Windscribe on-demand location-switching chain stays personal and out of this repo. (Renaming the repo from sshcustom-vpnchain is optional and your call — the Go module path can stay as-is.)

Each greyed control gets a summary like "Not available in REDIRECT mode."

Both points understood. Let me address the battery question first, then verify the VPN-chain design and lay out a plan.

Battery optimization via root — yes, and it changes my recommendation
Great instinct. And there's a key realization that makes this simpler: the tunnel does not drop when the app is killed. The SSH tunnel is held by the daemon — a separate root process. The app is just a control UI. So when HyperOS kills the app in the background, the tunnel keeps running; you only see a cosmetic flash because the UI cold-starts with a default state.

So instead of a heavy foreground service (A), I'd do the root-first combo:

Root battery-optimization exemption — the daemon (already root) whitelists the app on start: dumpsys deviceidle whitelist +com.sshcustom.vpnchain and cmd appops set ... RUN_ANY_IN_BACKGROUND allow. Keeps the UI process warmer so it's killed less often.
Seed last-known state (your option B) — persist the last state and show it instantly on cold start, so even if the OS does kill it, there's no "Stopped" flash.
That fully kills the visible flash without a permanent notification. My pick: root whitelist + B, skip the foreground service (A). (HyperOS's killer is aggressive beyond standard Doze, so the whitelist isn't a 100% guarantee — but B makes the flash invisible regardless.)

VPN Chain — verifying I understand your design
Let me play it back:

You connect SSHCustom first (SSH over the zero-rated bug host = free carrier path).
Then a VPN Chain tab runs OpenVPN using a Windscribe .ovpn config you download from their site.
Crucially, OpenVPN's connection to the Windscribe server must travel through the SSH tunnel (so the OpenVPN uplink is also free/zero-rated).
Net result: your exit IP becomes Windscribe's (your chosen location), and you can flip locations on demand by swapping configs.
So the chain is: apps → OpenVPN tun → OpenVPN → (through SSHCustom) → Windscribe → internet, exit IP = Windscribe. That matches what you said. ✓

One hard requirement to confirm: SSH only carries TCP, so you must use Windscribe's TCP OpenVPN configs (TCP 443/1194), not UDP — a UDP .ovpn can't ride the SSH tunnel. Can you confirm Windscribe gives you TCP configs? (They do.)

How I'd build it (the clean architecture)
The elegant way to force OpenVPN's uplink through SSH without loops:

Launch OpenVPN with socks-proxy 127.0.0.1 1081 injected → OpenVPN reaches the Windscribe server through the daemon's existing SOCKS5 (which forwards via SSH → bug host → free). No iptables gymnastics for the uplink; loopback is always direct.
OpenVPN brings up its own tun and becomes the default route for the device, so all apps exit via Windscribe.
Routing rules (reuse the owner-match pattern we already have): device traffic → OpenVPN tun; the SSH daemon's own traffic bypasses the OpenVPN tun (so SSH→bug-host stays on the real carrier link and doesn't loop); loopback stays direct.
So the only real network egress is SSH daemon → bug host (free), exactly as today.

Phased plan for VPN Chain (personal, its own tab)
Phase 1 — OpenVPN binary: build a static OpenVPN 2.6 (arm64-android) in CI (like we did for the daemon/tun2proxy) and ship it in the module's bin/. This is the main technical risk; I'll verify the build approach before committing (mbedTLS-based static build, or a vetted prebuilt).
Phase 2 — Module orchestration: an ovpn.service (start/stop OpenVPN with the chosen config + socks-proxy injection + management socket for status) and vpnchain.iptables (route device→OpenVPN tun, daemon bypass, loopback direct). Hard guard: VPN Chain can only start after SSHCustom is connected.
Phase 3 — App "VPN Chain" tab: import .ovpn files (file picker) and/or paste, list/select saved configs (your locations), Connect/Disconnect, and live status (state + exit IP so you can verify it's Windscribe). Gated on SSH being up.
Phase 4 — Exit-IP verification through the full chain, shown in the tab.
Sequencing — my suggestion
Ship the SSH polish first as one release (it's ready to implement): fix the ~20s disconnects (10s keepalive + remove the channel pool), drop tun/tun_udpgw, Settings tab last, gray-out toggles, and the root-battery + seed-state flash fix. Then tackle VPN Chain as its own focused effort (it's a meaty feature with the OpenVPN binary + routing + new tab).

So: (1) confirm Windscribe TCP configs, (2) confirm you're OK shipping the SSH polish first then VPN Chain second (or tell me to bundle), and (3) correct anything in the chain design above. Then say go and I'll start.

Everything confirmed. Let me answer the RAM question, fold in your config-folder detail, and lay out the complete bundled scope.

RAM — it stays small (the heavy part isn't persistent)
The key point: the always-running pieces are native root processes, not the app:

Go daemon (sshcustomd): ~8 MB (your own logs show mem=7–8 MB). It's capped but barely uses anything.
OpenVPN (only while VPN Chain is active): ~10–18 MB typically.
So the persistent footprint (SSH + chain running) is ~20–25 MB. Tiny.
The app UI itself: a Jetpack Compose app is ~90–140 MB while open in the foreground — that's just normal ART/Compose overhead for any modern Android app, not something we can shrink much. But since we're not adding a foreground service, when you leave the app it gets trimmed/killed and that RAM is reclaimed. The tunnel keeps running regardless (it's the 8 MB daemon).
So "the app doesn't use much RAM" in the way that matters: nothing heavy stays resident in the background. The root battery-whitelist just keeps the small footprint from being force-killed; it doesn't pin the 100 MB UI.

Config flow — using your existing folder (cleaner, no file picker)
Confirmed: configs live in /data/adb/sshcustom/vpnchain/configs/ (already shipped in the module). You paste .ovpn files there; the VPN Chain tab reads that directory (via root) and shows them in a dropdown to select. No import UI needed.

Looking at these logs, the pattern is very telling — and it points away from our app. Let me walk through it.

What actually happened (timeline)
20:53:13  payload sent → server response: HTTP/1.1 101 Switching Protocols   ← upgrade accepted
20:53:14  [tunnel] connected                                                  ← SSH up, apps work
   … ~39 seconds of working tunnel …
20:53:53  [tunnel] SSH connection lost: EOF                                   ← server/path closed it
20:53:56  payload sent → server response: HTTP/1.1 302 Found                  ← upgrade now REJECTED
20:53:56  connect failed: ssh handshake: EOF
   … every retry now gets 302, backing off 3→5→8→15→26s …
Two distinct things:

1. The ~40s drop (EOF). The connection came up, worked for ~39s, then the server/path closed it. Our new 10s keepalive actually helped here — it stretched the lifetime from the earlier ~20s to ~40s — but the close is being initiated by the far side, not by an idle timeout on our end (we were sending keepalives every 10s).

2. The real blocker — 101 became 302. This is the key. On the first attempt the WebSocket upgrade got 101 Switching Protocols (your bc.game→bridge→SSH path worked). After the drop, every reconnect gets 302 Found — an HTTP redirect — so there's no WebSocket, no SSH, and the handshake dies with EOF.

Our request is byte-for-byte identical every time (the log shows sent 87 bytes on every attempt, same payload, same Cloudflare IP). So the thing that changed is not on our side — it's the server/carrier responding differently. This is not an app or daemon bug; the daemon is doing exactly the right thing (same payload, correct exponential backoff 3→5→8→15→26s).

What's most likely causing the 302
A 302 instead of 101 means the WebSocket upgrade is no longer reaching/being accepted by your SSH-over-WS bridge. Two leading causes, both off-device:

Carrier anti-abuse (most likely): MTN zero-rates www.bc.game, lets the first connection through, then after ~40s of sustained tunnel traffic it kills the connection and starts injecting a 302 redirect (to a portal/notice page) for further requests to that host. This is a classic fair-use/anti-tunnel tactic on free hosts, and it usually clears after a cooldown.
Your origin bridge (the VPS behind ionosuk.techychi.xyz): if the WS→SSH bridge dropped your session and isn't re-accepting upgrades (crash, restart, max-connections, per-IP limit), Cloudflare falls back to serving bc.game's normal site, which 302-redirects HTTP→HTTPS.
The "Error / ssh handshake failed: EOF" you see on the Home page is just the app faithfully reflecting these repeated failures — expected, not a separate bug.

What I can do on our side (diagnostics, not a connectivity fix)
I can't make a 302 into a tunnel — that's the server/carrier's call — but I can make the app tell you exactly what's happening instead of the cryptic "ssh handshake: EOF":

Reject non-success upgrades clearly: when the payload response isn't 101/2xx, fail fast with a readable error like "WebSocket upgrade rejected — server returned HTTP 302 (bridge down or host throttled)" instead of proceeding into a doomed SSH handshake.
Log the 302's Location header — that single line would tell us where it redirects (a carrier portal URL = carrier throttling; an https://bc.game URL = Cloudflare/origin fallback). Right now I only log the status line.
Longer backoff after repeated rejections so we don't hammer the host while it's throttling (which can prolong a carrier block).
What to check on your side (this is where the fix is)
Wait ~5–10 min and reconnect. If 101 comes back, it's carrier throttling on sustained use — that's the behavior to work around (e.g., a different/rotating bug host).
Check your VPS bridge (ionosuk.techychi.xyz): is it up, does it drop sessions after ~40s, any per-IP/connection limit? Restart it and see if 101 resumes immediately.
Try a different bug host / SNI if the carrier is specifically throttling www.bc.game.
Want me to implement the diagnostics (#1–#3) so the next logs show the 302 Location and a clear reason? That'll pinpoint carrier-vs-bridge definitively. Say go and I'll add it; otherwise tell me what you find after a cooldown/bridge check.

1 tool

Cleaned up. Here's the detailed analysis — and it reframes the whole problem.

The core finding: proven injectors don't reimplement SSH — they drive the real OpenSSH client
I read the entire HTTP-CUSTOM-HEADERS-VPN codebase. Its pipeline is completely different from ours, and that difference is exactly why your identical config is rock-solid there and flaky here.

How the working injector actually connects (3 pieces)
1. A local "inject" proxy (tunnel.py + inject.py) — listens on a local port. Its payload-response handler is the critical bit:

def get_resp(self, server, client):
    packet = server.recv(2048)
    status = packet.decode(...).split('
')[0]
    if status.split('-')[0] == 'SSH':        # got the real SSH banner → forward, done
        return client.send(packet)
    else:                                     # ANY HTTP response (101/200/302/…)
        client.send(b'HTTP/1.1 200 Ok\r
\r
')   # fake-ack the local CONNECT
        return self.get_resp(server, client)  # …and KEEP READING until 'SSH-'
It ignores the HTTP status entirely and keeps reading/discarding responses until the server sends the literal SSH-... banner. The payload is treated as throwaway carrier-bait; the client just waits for SSH.

2. The real OpenSSH client (ssh.py) does the actual session:

proxycmd = '-o "ProxyCommand=nc -X CONNECT -x 127.0.0.1:<injectport> %h %p"'   # or corkscrew
sshcmd  = f'sshpass -p {password} ssh {proxycmd} -F configFile host1'
# … ssh -CND 1080 -o ConnectTimeout=3 -o StrictHostKeyChecking=no …
So OpenSSH connects through the local inject proxy (via an HTTP CONNECT), and exposes a dynamic SOCKS proxy on -D 1080. OpenSSH owns the SSH protocol — keepalives, rekeying, banner handling, reconnection — all the mature, battle-tested logic.

3. redsocks + iptables (proxification) — iptables redirects all device TCP to redsocks (127.0.0.1:8123), which converts it to SOCKS5 → OpenSSH's -D 1080. dns2socks handles DNS.

Our daemon vs. the proven approach
Proven injector	Our daemon
SSH engine	Real OpenSSH	Go x/crypto/ssh reimplementation
Payload response	Loop: discard every HTTP response until the SSH- banner; status ignored	Drain one HTTP block, then hand to SSH
Uplink	OpenSSH -D SOCKS	Go SOCKS5 + transparent proxy + DialTCP
Routing	redsocks → SOCKS	our transparent/tproxy → Go SSH
Why this explains your 40s-drop + 302 exactly
The ~40s drop: OpenSSH keeps these hostile, WebSocket-bridged carrier paths alive (its keepalive/rekey/connection handling is far more robust than the Go library). Our Go SSH client drops at ~20–40s. That's the difference — not the carrier.
The 302 storm: because our Go client drops, it reconnects, and the reconnect lands in a transient 302 state. The injector never drops, so it never reconnects into that — the connection just stays up. Same server, same payload; the only variable is the client holding the line.
Our single-block drain also proceeds into SSH after one HTTP response, so a 302-then-close becomes the cryptic "ssh handshake: EOF." The injector would just keep waiting for SSH-.
So: our hand-rolled Go SSH + one-shot drain is the fragile part. The fix isn't another patch — it's to do what the apps do: drive the real SSH client.

The "one or two" to pick up
Read-until-SSH- (the get_resp loop). Treat the payload as disposable; discard all HTTP responses regardless of status and wait for the real SSH banner. Never proceed to SSH on a non-SSH response.
Use the real SSH client (OpenSSH) instead of x/crypto/ssh — this is the big one, and almost certainly the root of your stability gap.
Proposed re-architecture — "an SSH daemon that works exactly like the apps"
Make the daemon orchestrate real OpenSSH, not reimplement it:

Ship the OpenSSH client (arm64) in the module. We already build OpenSSL for the OpenVPN job, so we reuse it; add a tiny sshpass for password auth. (Dropbear was tempting but its dbclient lacks -D dynamic SOCKS, which we need.)
Daemon role A — inject ProxyCommand proxy: a small local CONNECT proxy (Go) that dials the bug host (optionally TLS+SNI), sends the payload, then runs the read-until-SSH- loop, then raw-relays. OpenSSH uses it via -o ProxyCommand.
Daemon role B — launch & supervise OpenSSH: ssh -N -D <socksPort> -o ProxyCommand=… -o ServerAliveInterval=10 -o ServerAliveCountMax=3 -o ExitOnForwardFailure=yes … user@bughost -p <port> (password via sshpass/SSH_ASKPASS). OpenSSH provides the SOCKS uplink and keeps the line alive.
Routing — reuse what we have: keep the redirect/tproxy iptables, but the transparent proxy now forwards to OpenSSH's SOCKS (a SOCKS5 client dial) instead of Go DialTCP. No redsocks needed — our transparent proxy already reads the original destination. (VPN Chain still chains OpenVPN onto this same SOCKS.)
Modes map 1:1 to the apps: direct / payload / SNI(TLS) / payload+SNI.
Net effect: every user's HTTP-Injector/HTTP-Custom config "just works," because under the hood it is the same OpenSSH + payload-CONNECT flow — you're simply not using VpnService (we route via root iptables instead).

Honest notes
This is a real rework of the daemon's core (SSH engine + uplink), but it's the change that makes it dependable for a public release rather than another targeted patch.
Risk areas I'll verify during build: cross-compiling the OpenSSH client (+sshpass) for arm64-android (continue-on-error like OpenVPN so it can't block a release), and OpenSSH creating its SOCKS under Android/root.
The VPN Chain feature stays intact (it already chains onto the SOCKS uplink).
If you want this, say go and I'll implement it as the next release: OpenSSH client in the module, the inject-ProxyCommand proxy + OpenSSH supervisor in the daemon, the transparent→SOCKS bridge, and read-until-SSH-banner. Or tell me to start with just pick #1 (the get_resp loop) if you'd rather stage it.

Measurements complete and the workspace is clean. Here's the evidence-based comparison — I've separated what I measured from what I infer, and I'm explicit about what can't be measured without your physical device.

How I measured (so you can trust/repeat it)
Crypto throughput — ran the actual libraries each side uses: Go's x/crypto (our daemon) via a Go benchmark, and openssl speed 3.5.5 (what OpenSSH links against), on this host (Intel Xeon 8488C, AES‑NI/AVX).
Runtime memory floor — built a minimal Go program and a minimal C program, ran both, read /proc/<pid>/status RSS.
Binary sizes — from the actual v2.1.1 release ZIP and the host ssh binary.
Device RAM — from your own screenshots/logs (the real daemon on the real phone).
Measured numbers
Symmetric crypto (single core, this Xeon):

Cipher (SSH uses these)	Go x/crypto (our daemon)	OpenSSL (OpenSSH)
ChaCha20‑Poly1305 (SSH default)	1,947 MB/s	~3,514 MB/s
AES‑256‑GCM	4,854 MB/s	~10,400 MB/s
X25519 key exchange	13,060 ops/s	(equivalent class)
ed25519 verify	23,162 ops/s	(equivalent class)
→ OpenSSL is ~1.8–2.1× faster here (AVX‑512/VAES on this Xeon). But both are multiple GB/s.

Runtime floor (idle process):

Go (our daemon's runtime)	C (OpenSSH's runtime)
RSS floor	1.59 MB, 7 threads	0.27 MB, 1 thread
Sizes & real RAM:

Go daemon binary: 6.7 MB (one self‑contained binary). Host ssh client: 848 KB (but dynamically linked to libcrypto).
Real daemon RSS on your device: ~8–16 MB (your screen showed "Mem 15.8 MB").
What the numbers actually mean (per dimension)
1. Speed (tunnel throughput) — effectively a TIE, and neither is the bottleneck. This is the key proven point: even the slower engine (Go ChaCha20 at 1,947 MB/s) is ~15 Gbit/s. Your 5G real‑world link is, generously, 50–600 Mbit/s (≈6–75 MB/s). At 600 Mbit/s the cipher consumes ~3.9% of one core (Go) vs ~2.1% (OpenSSL) — at typical speeds it's well under 1% for both. The cellular link is the bottleneck for both; the cipher choice changes real tunnel speed by ~0%. The 2× crypto gap is invisible in practice.

2. Battery — effectively a TIE, radio‑dominated. Battery on a phone tunnel is dominated by the radio (same bytes over the air for both) and screen, not the cipher. The crypto CPU cost is the <1–4% of a core shown above, scaled by how much data you move — a rounding error in mAh terms. The only Go‑specific draw is its GC waking periodically, which is tiny at this heap size. Conclusion: no meaningful battery difference from the SSH engine. (Inferred from the measured CPU‑per‑byte + how phone power works; I can't give you mAh without your device + a power profiler.)

3. Memory — our current single Go daemon is actually LEANER than the OpenSSH design. Go's runtime floor is +1.3 MB over C, and the real daemon sits at ~8–16 MB. But the OpenSSH re‑architecture doesn't replace the Go daemon — it adds processes: you'd still run the Go daemon (API, metrics, the inject‑ProxyCommand proxy, iptables, VPN Chain) plus ssh (~5–7 MB with libcrypto) plus sshpass. So the OpenSSH path is ~20 MB total vs ~15 MB now — i.e., switching to OpenSSH is a memory regression, not a win.

4. Disk/APK‑ZIP size — OpenSSH adds ~2–3 MB. Today: one 6.7 MB Go daemon. OpenSSH path: Go daemon (~unchanged) + static ssh + libcrypto + sshpass ≈ +2–3 MB in the module ZIP.

5. Handshake / reconnect cost — TIE (network‑bound). A full SSH handshake is a few sub‑millisecond crypto ops (X25519 + one verify, both measured fast) wrapped in ~3–5 network round‑trips. Both engines do the same RTTs, so connect time is network‑bound and equal.

6. Stability / connection robustness — the ONE real difference, and it favors OpenSSH (proven by your logs). This is the only axis where they actually diverge: OpenSSH‑based injectors hold the line for hours on your carrier path; our Go client drops at ~20–40 s and then trips the 302 on reconnect. That's about maturity of connection handling (keepalive/rekey/edge‑case tolerance), not speed, battery, CPU, or memory.

The honest bottom line
On speed, battery, CPU, memory, and size, the two are equivalent for a phone tunnel — and on memory/disk our current Go daemon is actually the leaner option. The performance framing is essentially a wash because the cellular link, not the cipher, sets the ceiling for both.

The only reason to move to OpenSSH is stability/compatibility — making arbitrary users' configs "just work" the way the apps do. That's a real and important reason for a public release, but it's a robustness argument, not a performance one. So you genuinely have two defensible paths:

A) Adopt OpenSSH → maximum compatibility/stability (proven), at a small cost: +2–3 MB ZIP, ~+5 MB RAM, more moving parts (extra binary + sshpass + ProxyCommand plumbing).
B) Fix our Go client's connection handling (keepalive/rekey/reconnect tuning + the read‑until‑SSH‑banner behavior) → keeps the lean single‑binary footprint; risk is we may not perfectly match OpenSSH's tolerance on every hostile carrier path.
What I could NOT measure (so I won't claim it)
Real arm64 throughput on your SoC (this was amd64; on arm64 both use ARMv8 crypto extensions, so the gap is likely smaller, but still GB/s either way).
Actual battery mAh (needs your device + power profiler) — though the CPU‑per‑byte math shows it's negligible for both.
Real single‑stream tunnel speed on your link (network‑bound; would need an on‑device speedtest through each).
Tell me which path you want — A (OpenSSH, stability‑first) or B (fix the Go client, footprint‑first) — and I'll proceed. Given the data, my honest recommendation: if rock‑solid "works like the apps for everyone" is the goal for a public release, A is worth the few MB; if you value the lean single‑binary and are willing to iterate, B is viable.

Cleaned up. Here's the full, evidence-based report — I read every Go file in all three repos.

The single most important finding
boring uses the exact same golang.org/x/crypto/ssh library we do — and it's a polished, production-grade reconnecting tunnel manager. That proves staying on Go is the right call: our instability is not an inherent weakness of the Go SSH library. It's a lifecycle/handling gap. The difference between "the apps stay connected" and "ours visibly drops" is mostly that VpnService apps reconnect transparently under a tun that never goes down, while our daemon tears down iptables on every blip and makes the drop visible. So the fix is to handle reconnects the way boring (and VpnService) do — not to switch engines.

Now each project, what it does, and exactly what we take.

1. dataddo/sshrelay (360-line SSH server)
What it is: a locked-down bastion server (uses gliderlabs/ssh). It refuses shells and only allows direct-tcpip (local port forwarding):

ChannelHandlers: map[string]ssh.ChannelHandler{
    "direct-tcpip": ssh.DirectTCPIPHandler,   // port-forward only
    "session":      ssh.DefaultSessionHandler, // just prints "Only port forwarding available"
},
LocalPortForwardingCallback: func(...) bool { return true },
PublicKeyHandler: ...,   // per-user authorized keys
Relevance to us: low — we're a client, not a server. Takeaways: (a) the clean "forward-only, no shell" lockdown pattern if you ever ship your own server side; (b) gliderlabs/ssh is the go-to for Go SSH servers; (c) graceful signal-driven listener close. Nothing to port into our client. (One stone turned, mostly empty.)

2. rgzr/sshtun (client port-forward library)
What it is: a small library that opens a local listener and forwards each accepted connection over ssh.Client.Dial. Key detail in addConn/removeConn:

if tun.active == 0 { tun.sshClient = ssh.Dial(...) }  // lazy connect on first conn
...
if tun.active == 0 { tun.sshClient.Close() }          // close when idle
Critically: it has NO SSH keepalive and NO internal reconnect — Start() blocks and returns the error; the caller is expected to loop and restart. Relevance/takeaway: mostly a negative lesson — it's less robust than what we already have (we at least keep the connection warm and send keepalives). Its only nice idea is the clean ConnState/TunneledConnState callback model for surfacing state to a UI. Don't adopt its lazy-dial model (wrong for an always-on tunnel).

3. alebeck/boring (the gold mine — full reconnecting tunnel daemon)
This is the one to learn from. It's a daemon (internal/daemon/daemon.go) managing multiple tunnels over a unix socket, each tunnel in internal/tunnel/tunnel.go. Same x/crypto/ssh as us. The patterns worth taking:

(a) Transparent reconnect — the big one. In run(), on an unexpected disconnect it does not mark the tunnel dead or tear down its routing — it keeps it logically "Open" and calls reconnectLoop(); only an explicit stop closes it:

select {
case <-t.stop:                 // user asked to stop → really close
    stopped = true; t.client.Close()
case <-disconn:                 // SSH died → fall through to reconnect
}
t.listener.Close(); t.wg.Wait()
if !stopped {
    if err := t.reconnectLoop(); err == nil { return }  // re-Open() underneath, stay alive
}
reconnectLoop(): first retry ~immediate, then exponential backoff 500ms → ×2 → cap 1min, overall 15min timeout.

(b) keepalive — same shape as ours: client.SendRequest("keepalive@golang.org", true, nil) every N s; on error, close the client to trigger reconnect. (They use keepalive@golang.org; we use keepalive@openssh.com — both are fine; the point is forcing a round-trip.)

(c) client.Wait() → disconnect channel to detect drops — we already do this.

(d) Tailscale's SOCKS5 (internal/proxy/socks5.go, BSD-3, vendored with attribution) — a complete, correct SOCKS5 including UDP ASSOCIATE. Ours is hand-rolled and TCP-only.

(e) Jump-host chaining via ssh.NewClientConn over a previous client's Dial (wrapClient) — relevant only if you ever want multi-hop SSH.

What we actually adopt (all Go, grounded in the above)
Pick #1 — Transparent reconnect (from boring). This is the fix for your visible disconnects. Today our tunnelLoop runs ssh.iptables disable on every SSH drop, then re-enables after reconnect — so each ~40s blip yanks all routing and apps see "offline." Restructure it like boring: enable iptables once on first connect, keep it (and the transparent/SOCKS listeners) up across auto-reconnects, re-establish only the SSH client underneath, and tear iptables down only on explicit Stop (or after the reconnect-timeout gives up). Net effect: a ~40s drop becomes a sub-2-second invisible stall — exactly the VpnService-app experience — even if we can't prevent every drop. (Bonus: keeping rules up is fail-closed = no traffic leak during the gap, which matters for a carrier-bypass tunnel.)

Pick #2 — TCP-level keepalive on the dial (what OpenSSH does, we don't). OpenSSH sets TCPKeepAlive yes by default; our dnsx dialer sets none. Add SO_KEEPALIVE (~15–30 s) on the underlying socket. This keeps the NAT/WebSocket path mapping alive at the TCP layer, independent of the SSH-level keepalive — a cheap, proven anti-idle-drop measure that may directly extend that ~40 s lifetime.

Pick #3 — Replace our SOCKS5 with Tailscale's (via boring). Drop-in a correct, UDP-capable SOCKS5 (BSD-3, attributed). Immediately benefits the VPN Chain (OpenVPN-over-SOCKS) and any future UDP, and removes edge-case bugs in our minimal implementation.

Pick #4 — read-until-SSH-banner payload handling (from the earlier injector study, reinforced here). Treat the payload as disposable carrier-bait: after sending it, discard any/all HTTP responses and wait for the literal SSH- banner before starting SSH, instead of proceeding after one HTTP block.

Align the backoff to boring's shape (immediate → ×2 → 1 min cap → 15 min give-up), which also avoids hammering the host into the 302 state.

What we deliberately do NOT do
Don't switch to OpenSSH (your call — last resort, noted).
Don't adopt sshtun's lazy-dial/close-on-idle (wrong for always-on).
Don't add a server (sshrelay) — we're a client.
Honest caveats (proven vs. expected)
Proven: boring runs a robust reconnecting tunnel on the same Go SSH lib → Go is viable; the SOCKS5 and reconnect patterns are real, working code we can lift.
Expected (not yet proven on your device): transparent-reconnect + TCP-keepalive should make drops invisible and may reduce their frequency — but if your carrier path forcibly closes every ~40 s regardless, you'll still get a brief periodic stall (just no "Error" screen). Only an on-device run after the change will confirm how often it actually drops. If, after this, it's still unacceptable, that's when OpenSSH becomes the last-resort lever.
If you want, say go and I'll implement Picks #1–#4 (transparent reconnect, TCP keepalive, Tailscale SOCKS5, read-until-banner) as the next Go-only release. I'd suggest doing #1 + #2 first (they target your exact symptom) and bundling #3/#4 in the same build.

a carrier "free bug-host" loophole (zero-rated host) via HTTP payload / WebSocket
upgrade injection. ALL device TCP is routed through the SSH tunnel via iptables
(NO Android VpnService — routing is done with root + iptables instead).

## 4. THE CORE UNRESOLVED PROBLEM (what we're actually trying to fix)
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
Tunnel connects fine (payload → 101 Switching Protocols → SSH connected → apps work)
but DROPS after ~20–40s with "[tunnel] SSH connection lost: EOF". On reconnect the
server starts returning "HTTP/1.1 302 Found" instead of 101, so the SSH handshake
fails ("ssh: handshake failed: EOF") repeatedly. The Home screen then shows "Error".

CRITICAL FACT: the IDENTICAL ssh server + payload + bug host works rock-solid on
HTTP Injector / HTTP Custom / SSHCustom (which use Android VpnService). So the
problem is HOW OUR DAEMON handles the connection, not the carrier/server.

REFRAME (proven by analyzing alebeck/boring, which uses the SAME golang.org/x/crypto/ssh
library and is a robust production tunnel): the Go SSH library is NOT the problem.
The VpnService apps reconnect TRANSPARENTLY (their tun never goes down; only the
underlying socket reconnects), so drops are invisible. OUR daemon tears down iptables
on EVERY drop → the user sees "disconnected". Also our reconnect can trip the carrier/
server into a 302 state.

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
## 5. THE PLAN WE WILL ADOPT NEXT (Go-only; user APPROVED staying on Go)
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
User decision: STICK WITH GO. **OpenSSH is a LAST RESORT — "last for real."** Do NOT
propose OpenSSH again unless these Go fixes are exhausted. We did a full measured
benchmark (see §8): Go vs OpenSSL are equivalent on speed/battery/RAM/size (both
crypto >> network speed; battery is radio-dominated); Go daemon is actually leaner.
OpenSSH only wins on connection STABILITY — which we'll instead fix in Go via:

PICK #1 (the big one — from boring/internal/tunnel/tunnel.go): TRANSPARENT RECONNECT.
  Restructure daemon tunnelLoop so iptables + the transparent/SOCKS/tproxy listeners
  are enabled ONCE on first connect and STAY UP across auto-reconnects. Re-establish
  ONLY the SSH client underneath. Tear down iptables ONLY on explicit Stop (or after a
  reconnect give-up timeout, e.g. 15 min). Fail-closed during the gap (no traffic leak).
  → turns a ~40s drop into a sub-2s invisible stall, matching the VpnService apps.

PICK #2 (from OpenSSH defaults, which we lack): TCP-LEVEL KEEPALIVE.
  Set SO_KEEPALIVE (~15–30s) on the underlying dial (in daemon/internal/dnsx dialer).
  OpenSSH sets TCPKeepAlive yes by default; we set none. Keeps NAT/WS path alive at TCP layer.

PICK #3 (from boring/internal/proxy/socks5.go — Tailscale's, BSD-3): replace our
  hand-rolled SOCKS5 with a correct, UDP-ASSOCIATE-capable SOCKS5. Benefits VPN Chain
  (OpenVPN-over-SOCKS) and future UDP. Vendor with attribution.

PICK #4 (from abdoxfox/HTTP-CUSTOM-HEADERS-VPN get_resp): READ-UNTIL-SSH-BANNER payload
  handling. After sending payload, discard ALL HTTP responses (any status) and keep
  reading until the literal "SSH-" banner appears; never proceed to SSH on a non-SSH
  response. (Proven injector behavior; more robust than our single-block drain.)

ALSO: align reconnect backoff to immediate→×2→1min cap→15min give-up. Keep SSH
keepalive at 10s. Suggested order: #1 + #2 first (target the symptom), bundle #3/#4.

(Reference projects analyzed in depth this session: dataddo/sshrelay [server, not
applicable], rgzr/sshtun [too minimal, no keepalive — negative lesson], alebeck/boring
[same x/crypto/ssh, robust reconnect + Tailscale SOCKS5 — the gold], abdoxfox/
HTTP-CUSTOM-HEADERS-VPN [openssh + ProxyCommand + get_resp loop + redsocks].)

## 6. VPN CHAIN FEATURE (v2.1.0 — PERSONAL, untested on device)
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
Goal: chain OpenVPN (Windscribe) THROUGH the SSH tunnel so the exit IP = Windscribe,
while OpenVPN's link to Windscribe rides the free bug-host path. PERSONAL feature — the
user does NOT want it in any public release; the public module is SSH-tunnel-only. (We
deferred the public/private split discussion — do not act on it yet.)
- VPN Chain tab: dropdown of .ovpn files from /data/adb/sshcustom/vpnchain/configs/,
  OpenVPN username/password fields, Connect/Disconnect, status, Windscribe exit IP
  (app fetches its own public IP via https://ipinfo.io/json — routes through the chain).
- module/scripts/ovpn.service: runs OpenVPN with `--socks-proxy 127.0.0.1:<socks>`
  (rides SSH SOCKS), `--dev tun0`, auth file /data/adb/sshcustom/vpnchain/auth.txt.
  On start: touch run/vpnchain.active marker → ssh.iptables goes "socks-only" (skips
  device redirect); then vpnchain.iptables routes apps→OpenVPN tun and BYPASSES the SSH
  daemon's own uplink (uid 0 → side table) to avoid loops. On stop: reverse it.
- module/scripts/vpnchain.iptables: the routing/bypass.
- CI builds static OpenVPN 2.5.9 (OpenSSL 3 + LZO, --enable-iproute2 IPROUTE=/system/bin/ip)
  for arm64-android; job is continue-on-error so it never blocks a release.
- REQUIRES a TCP Windscribe .ovpn (SSH carries TCP only). UNTESTED on device — routing
  will likely need on-device tuning.

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
## 8. MEASURED BENCHMARK EVIDENCE (Go vs OpenSSL — done this session)
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
On a Xeon 8488C (amd64, AES-NI/AVX), single core:
- Go x/crypto: ChaCha20-Poly1305 1947 MB/s; AES-256-GCM 4854 MB/s; X25519 13060 ops/s; ed25519 verify 23162 ops/s.
- OpenSSL 3.5.5: ChaCha20-Poly1305 ~3514 MB/s; AES-256-GCM ~10400 MB/s.
- Runtime RSS floor: Go minimal 1.59 MB / 7 threads; C minimal 0.27 MB / 1 thread.
- Real daemon RSS on device: ~8–16 MB. Daemon binary 6.7 MB; openvpn binary 4.25 MB.
Conclusion: both ciphers are GB/s (>> any 5G link ~50–600 Mbit/s), so cipher is NEVER
the tunnel bottleneck; battery is radio-dominated; Go daemon is leaner than an OpenSSH
hybrid. OpenSSH's only real edge is connection robustness → being fixed in Go (§5).

## 9. BUILD / TEST / RELEASE PROCESS (exact)
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

    cd /projects/sandbox/sshcustom-vpnchain/daemon
    GOOS=android GOARCH=arm64 CGO_ENABLED=0 go build -trimpath -buildvcs=false -ldflags="-s -w -X main.version=X.Y.Z" -o /tmp/test ./cmd/sshcustomd/

CANNOT build the APK locally (no Android SDK) — CI validates it.

Release flow:
  1. Bump VERSION file (e.g. 2.1.2).
  2. Commit changes (git add specific files; git commit).
  3. Create a local branch whose name EXACTLY matches what you'll push, then push with
     the github_push_to_remote TOOL (NOT bash git — gateway remote rejects bash git auth).
     The tool pushes the current local branch to remote_branch_name.
  4. Open/update PR with github_create_pull_request tool (target main).
  5. Push the TAG to trigger CI release (the branch-push tool CANNOT push tags). Use the PAT:
       TOKEN=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
       git -C <repo> tag -a vX.Y.Z HEAD -m "..."
       git -C <repo> remote add ghdirect "https://${TOKEN}@github.com/GoodyOG/sshcustom-vpnchain.git"
       git -C <repo> push ghdirect vX.Y.Z 2>&1 | sed "s/${TOKEN}/<redacted>/g"
       git -C <repo> remote remove ghdirect
     (Always redact the token in output; remove the temp remote after.)
  6. Monitor CI via GitHub API with the PAT (curl .../actions/runs and .../jobs).
     Tag push → run with event=push, head_branch=vX.Y.Z. Confirm "Create GitHub Release"
     succeeds and the release has both APK (~7.5MB) and ZIP (~4.2MB w/ openvpn).
CI on tag vX.Y.Z produces: SSHCustom-VPNChain-release.apk + SSHCustom-VPNChain-vX.Y.Z.zip
attached to the GitHub Release automatically.
Deployment: daemon/script changes → user must REFLASH the module ZIP; app changes → REINSTALL APK.

## 10. ENVIRONMENT GOTCHAS (will save you time)
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
- The bash tool name FLAPS between "run_command" and "execute_bash" — if one says
  "not available", use the other. NEVER use `cd` (unsupported) — use the cwd parameter.
- read_files with MULTIPLE paths frequently returns empty "{}"; even single-file reads
  sometimes return "{}". Workaround: read one file at a time / retry; str_replace still
  works (it matches actual file content) even when reads render empty.
- /tmp does NOT persist across separate bash invocations. Use /projects/sandbox/... paths.
- bash `git push`/`git fetch` to the gateway remote (origin) FAILS ("Missing AuthToken").
  Use the github_push_to_remote tool for branches; PAT→github.com for tags (see §9).
- github_push_to_remote may report a different headSha (it can re-create the commit).
- A ".kiro/skills submodule" warning appears in CI cleanup logs — harmless, ignore.
- Sandbox: OPEN_INTERNET; Go 1.25.1 + 1.23.8; openssl CLI installable via dnf.
